Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A convex polygon consisting of N vertices is given. The coordinates of each vertex are represented counterclockwise by (X1, Y1), (X2, Y2), ……, (XN, YN). No matter what straight line passes through the point P, find the coordinates of the point P so that the areas of the two convex polygons obtained after cutting are equal.

Constraints

* All inputs are integers

* 3 ≤ N ≤ 50

* 0 ≤ | Xi |, | Yi | ≤ 1000000

* The input polygon is a simple convex polygon.

* The output must satisfy max (| X-cX |, | Y-cY |) ≤ 0.0001 when the output coordinates are (X, Y) and the exact solution is (cX, cY).

Input

The input is given in the following format.

> N
> X1 Y1
> X2 Y2
> ……
> XN YN
>

Output

If there is a point that satisfies the condition of the problem statement, the coordinates of that point

> X Y
>

Output in the format of. If the point does not exist, output "NA" on one line.

Examples

Input

4
100 100
0 100
0 0
100 0


Output

50.00000 50.00000

3
100 100
0 100
0 0


Output

NA

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a convex polygon with N vertices given in counterclockwise order
2. We need to find a point P such that any straight line passing through P divides the polygon into two convex polygons of equal area
3. If such a point exists, output its coordinates; otherwise output "NA"

## Key Insights

1. For a convex polygon, if there exists a point P such that every line through P divides the area equally, then P must be the centroid (center of mass) of the polygon.

2. However, this is not always true. For a general convex polygon, the centroid is the point where the area is balanced in all directions, but we need to verify that every line through this point creates equal areas.

3. Actually, there's a geometric property: for a convex region, there exists a point such that every line through it divides the region into two equal areas if and only if the region has a center of symmetry. 

4. For a polygon to have such a point, it must have rotational symmetry of 180 degrees. This happens when we can find a point P such that for every vertex A, there's a corresponding vertex B such that P is the midpoint of A and B.

5. In a triangle (3 vertices), this is only possible if it's a degenerate case or has specific symmetry. The only triangle with such a property is an equilateral triangle, but even then, the centroid doesn't guarantee equal areas for all lines through it.

6. Actually, let me reconsider: for a convex polygon, if there exists a point such that every line through it divides the area equally, then that point must be the centroid. But the condition that every line creates equal areas is very restrictive.

7. More importantly, for a convex region, if every line through a point divides it into equal areas, then that point must be the center of symmetry. For a polygon, this happens when it is centrally symmetric (has 180-degree rotational symmetry).

8. For a triangle (N=3), no non-degenerate triangle has 180-degree rotational symmetry. So for a triangle, no such point exists.

9. For quadrilaterals, if it's a parallelogram (has 180-degree symmetry), then the center of symmetry works. For other quadrilaterals, such a point might not exist.

10. Actually, I think the property is that such a point exists if and only if the polygon is centrally symmetric. For a convex polygon to be centrally symmetric, there must be a point P such that for every vertex V, there's another vertex V' such that P is the midpoint of V and V'.

11. For a triangle: impossible to have central symmetry (3 is odd)
12. For a quadrilateral: possible if it's a parallelogram (opposite vertices symmetric)
13. For pentagons and above: impossible if N is odd (since odd number of vertices can't pair up for central symmetry)

## Algorithm

1. Check if N is odd. If yes, output "NA" (since we can't have central symmetry with odd number of vertices)
2. If N is even, check if the polygon is centrally symmetric
3. To check central symmetry: for each vertex i, find the opposite vertex (i+N/2) % N, and verify that the midpoint of vertex i and vertex (i+N/2) % N is the same for all i
4. If the polygon is centrally symmetric, then the center of symmetry is our answer
5. If not, output "NA"

Wait, let me reconsider the example:
- Example 1: quadrilateral (100,100), (0,100), (0,0), (100,0) - this is a rectangle, which is centrally symmetric. Its center is at (50,50). This matches the expected output.
- Example 2: triangle (100,100), (0,100), (0,0) - this is not centrally symmetric (N=3 is odd), so output "NA". This matches.

But is it true that for a convex polygon with N>3 and N even, if it's not centrally symmetric, such a point doesn't exist?

Actually, I think the key insight is: for a convex region, if there exists a point P such that every line through P divides the area in half, then P must be the centroid and the region must be centrally symmetric. This is a known geometric property.

However, I believe for any convex polygon, the centroid has the property that any line through it divides the polygon into two regions of equal area only if the polygon is symmetric. But actually, I think I'm mistaken.

Let me think again: the centroid doesn't guarantee equal areas for all lines. For example, a very elongated rectangle will have a centroid, but a line through the centroid at an angle will not necessarily create equal areas.

Actually, the correct property is: for a convex region, there exists a line through the centroid that divides the area equally, but there may not exist a point such that ALL lines through it divide the area equally.

The condition that EVERY line through P divides the area equally is extremely strong. This happens only when the region has central symmetry.

For central symmetry: if there exists a point P such that for every point X in the polygon, the point P + (X-P) rotated by 180 degrees is also in the polygon.

For a polygon with vertices, this means for each vertex Vi, there should be a vertex Vj such that P is the midpoint of Vi and Vj.

Since vertices are given, we can check this:

1. If N is odd, impossible to have central symmetry (each vertex needs a partner), so output "NA"
2. If N is even, for each i, the vertex opposite to i should be at (i + N/2) % N, and the midpoint of vi and v(i+N/2)%N should be the same for all i

If this condition holds for all i, then the midpoint is our answer.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    vertices = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        vertices.append((x, y))
    
    # If N is odd, no central symmetry possible
    if n % 2 == 1:
        print("NA")
        return
    
    # Check if the polygon is centrally symmetric
    # For each vertex i, its opposite should be at (i + n//2) % n
    # and the midpoint of i and (i + n//2) % n should be the same for all i
    
    center_x, center_y = 0.0, 0.0
    
    valid = True
    for i in range(n // 2):
        j = i + n // 2
        x1, y1 = vertices[i]
        x2, y2 = vertices[j]
        
        # Calculate midpoint
        mid_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
        mid_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
        
        # If this is the first pair, set center
        if i == 0:
            center_x, center_y = mid_x, mid_y
        else:
            # All midpoints should be the same
            if abs(center_x - mid_x) > 1e-9 or abs(center_y - mid_y) > 1e-9:
                valid = False
                break
    
    if valid:
        print(f"{center_x:.6f} {center_y:.6f}")
    else:
        print("NA")

solve()
```

Key observations:
1. For any line through P to divide the polygon into two equal areas, P must be the centroid of the polygon
2. The centroid (center of mass) of a polygon has the property that any line through it divides the polygon into two equal areas (this is a known geometric property)
3. However, for a triangle (N=3), it's not true that any line through the centroid divides it into equal areas - only specific lines do

Let me think more carefully...

Actually, the key insight is that for a convex polygon, if a point P has the property that every line through it divides the polygon into two equal areas, then P must be the centroid. 

But is this property true for every convex polygon? Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: Square (4 vertices) with vertices at (100,100), (0,100), (0,0), (100,0)
- The centroid should be at (50,50)
- This matches the expected output

Example 2: Triangle (3 vertices) with vertices at (100,100), (0,100), (0,0)
- The centroid would be at ((100+0+0)/3, (100+100+0)/3) = (33.33, 66.67)
- But the expected output is "NA"

So why is the triangle case "NA"? 

The key insight: For a triangle, a line through the centroid will not necessarily divide it into two equal areas. In fact, only specific lines (those parallel to a side, passing through centroid) will do this.

The property that "any line through P divides the polygon into equal areas" is extremely strong. For a triangle, this is not true for the centroid. In fact, I believe this property only holds for centrally symmetric polygons (like squares, rectangles, parallelograms, etc.).

For centrally symmetric polygons, the centroid has this property. For non-centrally symmetric polygons like triangles, no such point exists.

So the algorithm:
1. Check if the polygon is centrally symmetric (has a center of symmetry)
2. If it is centrally symmetric, return the centroid
3. If it is not, return "NA"

Wait, let me reconsider. Is there a simpler characterization?

Actually, for any convex polygon, if there exists a point P such that every line through P divides the polygon into equal areas, then P must be the centroid OF THE POLYGON. But the property that "every line through P" gives equal areas is very strong.

For a triangle, if you draw a line through centroid parallel to one side, it divides it into areas in ratio 1:2 (not equal). So the property doesn't hold.

For a parallelogram, the centroid has the property that lines through it will divide the area equally? Actually, I'm not sure.

Let me think differently. The property "every line through P divides the area equally" implies that the polygon is symmetric with respect to P. This means P must be the center of symmetry of the polygon.

So the algorithm:
1. Find if the polygon has a center of symmetry
2. If it does, return that center
3. If it doesn't, return "NA"

How to check for center of symmetry?
For each pair of opposite points, their midpoint should be the same for all pairs.

For a convex polygon with N vertices (counterclockwise), we can check if there exists a point C such that for every vertex V_i, there exists a vertex V_j such that C is the midpoint of V_i and V_j, and the pairing is consistent.

For a symmetric polygon, each vertex should have a diametrically opposite vertex.

For odd N, this is impossible (no pairing of vertices), so no center of symmetry exists.

For even N, we can check if there's a point C such that for each i, (V_i + V_{i+N/2}) / 2 = C.

Actually, for a convex polygon to have center of symmetry, N must be even, and for each i, the midpoint of (V_i, V_{i+N/2}) should be the same.

Let me verify with the square:
- Vertices: (100,100), (0,100), (0,0), (100,0)
- N=4, so N/2 = 2
- Pair 0 with 2: ((100+0)/2, (100+0)/2) = (50,50)
- Pair 1 with 3: ((0+100)/2, (100+0)/2) = (50,50)
- Same midpoint, so it has center of symmetry at (50,50)

For a triangle (N=3, odd), it's impossible to have center of symmetry, so no such point exists.

For a more general convex polygon, if N is odd, it cannot have center of symmetry (since vertices can't be paired), so answer is "NA".

For even N, we check if all pairs of opposite vertices have the same midpoint.

```python
n = int(input())
vertices = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    vertices.append((x, y))

# If N is odd, no center of symmetry exists
if n % 2 == 1:
    print("NA")
else:
    # N is even, check if all pairs of opposite vertices have the same midpoint
    n_half = n // 2
    mid_x, mid_y = None, None
    
    # Get midpoint of first pair (vertex 0 and vertex n_half)
    v0, v_half = vertices[0], vertices[n_half]
    mid_x = (v0[0] + v_half[0]) / 2.0
    mid_y = (v0[1] + v_half[1]) / 2.0
    
    # Check if all other pairs have the same midpoint
    valid = True
    for i in range(1, n_half):
        v_i = vertices[i]
        v_i_plus_half = vertices[i + n_half]
        current_x = (v_i[0] + v_i_plus_half[0]) / 2.0
        current_y = (v_i[1] + v_i_plus_half[1]) / 2.0
        
        if abs(current_x - mid_x) > 1e-9 or abs(current_y - mid_y) > 1e-9:
            valid = False
            break
    
    if valid:
        print(f"{mid_x:.6f} {mid_y:.6f}")
    else:
        print("NA")
```

Wait, I need to be careful. In a convex polygon, for it to have center of symmetry, the vertex pairing should be consistent. The standard pairing is (0, n/2), (1, n/2+1), etc.

But is this the only possibility? For a general convex polygon, the center of symmetry would have to map vertices to other vertices in a consistent way. The pairing above is what's typically used.

However, let me reconsider: is it possible to have a center of symmetry where vertices are paired differently?

For a convex polygon with center of symmetry, if you rotate by 180 degrees around the center, each vertex maps to another vertex. Since the vertices are given in counterclockwise order, this rotation should map vertex i to vertex (i + n/2) mod n.

Therefore, the algorithm above should work.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a point P such that any straight line passing through P divides a convex polygon into two regions of equal area.

Let me analyze the examples:

**Example 1**: A square with vertices (100,100), (0,100), (0,0), (100,0)
- The center is at (50,50)
- Any line through (50,50) should divide the square into two equal areas
- This works because of symmetry

**Example 2**: A right triangle with vertices (100,100), (0,100), (0,0)
- This is a triangle with base 100 and height 100
- The centroid is at (33.3, 66.7), but lines through centroid don't always divide area equally
- The answer is "NA", meaning no such point exists

## Key Insight

For a convex polygon, there exists a point P such that every line through P divides the polygon into two equal areas if and only if the polygon has a center of symmetry.

For a convex polygon, this happens when the polygon is centrally symmetric, meaning for every vertex (xi,yi), there exists another vertex (xi',yi') such that the midpoint is the same center point.

In particular:
- A parallelogram has a center of symmetry
- A square, rectangle, rhombus, etc. have centers of symmetry
- A triangle generally does not have a center of symmetry (except in degenerate cases)

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the total area of the polygon using the shoelace formula
2. Check if the polygon is centrally symmetric by finding if there's a center point such that for every vertex (xi,yi), the point symmetric about the center is also a vertex
3. If it is centrally symmetric, that center point is our answer
4. If not, output "NA"

## Steps to check central symmetry:

1. First, find the centroid of the polygon (which is the average of all vertex coordinates)
2. For each vertex (xi,yi), compute its symmetric point about the centroid: (2*cx - xi, 2*cy - yi)
3. Check if this symmetric point is also a vertex of the polygon (possibly at a different index)
4. If all symmetric points are vertices, then the centroid is the center point

Note: Due to floating point precision, we need to be careful with comparisons.

Let me implement this:

```python
def cross_product(o, a, b):
    return (a[0] - o[0]) * (b[1] - o[1]) - (a[1] - o[1]) * (b[0] - o[0])

def polygon_area(vertices):
    n = len(vertices)
    area = 0.0
    for i in range(n):
        j = (i + 1) % n
        area += vertices[i][0] * vertices[j][1]
        area -= vertices[j][0] * vertices[i][1]
    return abs(area) / 2.0

def are_equal(a, b, eps=1e-9):
    return abs(a - b) < eps

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    vertices = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        vertices.append((x, y))
    
    # Calculate total area
    total_area = polygon_area(vertices)
    
    # If area is 0, it's degenerate - but constraints say N >= 3, so should be non-zero
    if total_area == 0:
        print("NA")
        return
    
    # Calculate centroid
    cx = sum(v[0] for v in vertices) / n
    cy = sum(v[1] for v in vertices) / n
    
    # Check if the polygon is centrally symmetric around (cx, cy)
    # For each vertex (x,y), its symmetric point should be another vertex
    symmetric_vertices = set()
    
    for i in range(n):
        x, y = vertices[i]
        x_sym = 2 * cx - x
        y_sym = 2 * cy - y
        symmetric_vertices.add((round(x_sym, 10), round(y_sym, 10)))
    
    # Check if all symmetric points exist in the original vertices
    # We need to check if each symmetric point is exactly one of the vertices
    # (accounting for floating point precision)
    
    # Create a set of all vertices with tolerance
    vertex_set = set()
    for x, y in vertices:
        vertex_set.add((round(x, 10), round(y, 10)))
    
    # Verify every symmetric point is in the vertex set
    valid = True
    for x_sym, y_sym in symmetric_vertices:
        if not any(are_equal(x_sym, vx) and are_equal(y_sym, vy) for vx, vy in vertex_set):
            valid = False
            break
    
    # Also need to check that the symmetric point is actually in the original list
    # The symmetric point should be exactly one of the vertices in the original polygon
    if not valid:
        print("NA")
        return
    
    # For a convex polygon, if it's centrally symmetric, the centroid is the center point
    # And any line through this point will divide the area equally
    print(f"{cx:.6f} {cy:.6f}")

solve()
```

Wait, I need to reconsider. The condition for central symmetry is that each vertex has a symmetric counterpart. But the symmetric point of a vertex might not be another vertex - it might be a different point.

Actually, the key insight is that for a convex polygon to have the property that every line through a point P divides it into two equal areas, the point P must be the centroid, and the polygon must be centrally symmetric about that centroid.

But let me think about triangles. A triangle has 3 vertices, so for central symmetry, we'd need:
- If A has symmetric point B, then B's symmetric point should be A, so the midpoint of A and B is the center
- Similarly for A and C, B and C

For a triangle with vertices (0,0), (0,100), (100,100), the centroid is (33.33, 33.33). The symmetric of (0,0) would be (66.66, 66.66), which is not a vertex. So no central symmetry.

For a square: vertices (0,0), (0,100), (100,100), (100,0). The centroid is (50,50). 
- Symmetric of (0,0) is (100,100) ✓
- Symmetric of (0,100) is (100,0) ✓
- Symmetric of (100,100) is (0,0) ✓
- Symmetric of (100,0) is (0,100) ✓

So it works.

Therefore, my algorithm is correct:

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    vertices = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        vertices.append((x, y))
    
    # Calculate centroid
    cx = sum(v[0] for v in vertices) / n
    cy = sum(v[1] for v in vertices) / n
    
    # Check if the polygon is centrally symmetric around (cx, cy)
    # For each vertex (x,y), its symmetric point is (2*cx - x, 2*cy - y)
    # This symmetric point should also be one of the vertices
    
    # Convert vertices to a set with small tolerance for floating point comparison
    vertex_set = set()
    for x, y in vertices:
        # Round to avoid floating point errors
        vertex_set.add((round(x, 10), round(y, 10)))
    
    # Check each vertex - its symmetric point should be in the set
    valid = True
    for x, y in vertices:
        x_sym = 2 * cx - x
        y_sym = 2 * cy - y
        # Round for comparison
        x_sym_round = round(x_sym, 10)
        y_sym_round = round(y_sym, 10)
        
        # Check if (x_sym_round, y_sym_round) exists in vertex_set
        found = False
        for vx, vy in vertex_set:
            if abs(x_sym_round - vx) < 1e-9 and abs(y_sym_round - vy) < 1e-9:
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            valid = False
            break
    
    if valid:
        print(f"{cx:.6f} {cy:.6f}")
    else:
        print("NA")

solve()
```

    for x, y in vertices:

Actually, the correct geometric principle is: For any convex body, if a line through a point divides the area into two equal parts, that point is called an area bisector point. The existence of such a point is guaranteed for any convex body, and in fact, there are infinitely many such points.

But the problem says "No matter what straight line passes through the point P" - meaning every possible line through P should divide the area into two equal parts.

This is an extremely strong condition. For this to happen, P must be such that every direction gives equal division.

This would only happen if the polygon is symmetric and P is the center of symmetry. For example, in a square or circle, the center point has this property.

In a triangle (which has no center of symmetry), no such point exists.

Indeed, in the triangle example, the output is "NA", which suggests that for non-symmetric convex polygons, no such point exists.

So the solution:
1. Check if the polygon has rotational or central symmetry
2. If it has central symmetry (i.e., it's symmetric under 180-degree rotation), then the center of symmetry is the answer
3. Otherwise, "NA"

But wait, the square in example 1 has central symmetry - each point (x,y) has a symmetric point (100-x, 100-y) relative to the center (50,50).

For a convex polygon to have the property that every line through a point divides it into equal areas, that point must be the center of symmetry.

This is because for different directions, the division of area would depend on the symmetry. If there's no central symmetry, then there's no point where every line through it gives equal areas.

Therefore, the algorithm:

1. Read the vertices
2. Check if the polygon has central symmetry (180-degree rotational symmetry)
3. If it does, compute the center of symmetry
4. If it doesn't, output "NA"

For central symmetry, for each vertex (xi, yi), there should be a corresponding vertex (cX - xi, cY - yi) such that when we rotate the polygon 180 degrees around (cX, cY), we get the same polygon.

The center of symmetry (cX, cY) should be the midpoint of any pair of symmetric vertices.

So I can take the first vertex and find its symmetric counterpart by finding the point (x', y') such that the midpoint is (cX, cY), which would be the center.

But since vertices are given counterclockwise, the symmetric vertex of (xi, yi) should be the vertex that is opposite in the cycle.

Actually, a necessary and sufficient condition is that when we rotate the polygon 180 degrees around some point (cX, cY), we get the same polygon.

- For each vertex i, the point (cX - xi, cY - yi) should be equal to some vertex j
- All vertices should form pairs that are symmetric about (cX, cY)

For central symmetry in a polygon with odd N, each vertex has a unique opposite, and no vertex is fixed.

So for odd N:
- The number of vertices must be even, because each vertex pairs with another
- So N must be even for central symmetry

For even N:
- Vertices are paired: vertex i and vertex (i+N/2) % N should be symmetric about the center

For odd N:
- Since each vertex needs a symmetric partner, and there are odd number of vertices, one vertex would have to be self-symmetric (map to itself under 180° rotation)
- But under 180° rotation, a point maps to (cx - x, cy - y)
- For a point to map to itself, we need (cx - x, cy - y) = (x, y), so x = cx, y = cy, which means the point is at the center
- But vertices can't be at the center unless they're duplicated
- And since no vertex can be at the center (it would have to be paired with itself), this is impossible for odd N

Therefore:
- Central symmetry is only possible for even N
- For even N, check if each vertex i has its symmetric counterpart at vertex (i + N/2) % N
- The center is the midpoint of any pair of symmetric vertices

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: Square with vertices (100,100), (0,100), (0,0), (100,0)
- N = 4 (even)

Therefore, the algorithm:

1. Read input
2. If N is odd, output "NA"
3. If N is even:
   a. For each i from 0 to N/2 - 1, check if the midpoint of vertex i and vertex (i + N/2) % N is the same for all pairs
   b. If all pairs have the same midpoint, that's our answer
   c. Otherwise, output "NA"

Wait, is it possible for even N to have central symmetry but not with pairing (i, i+N/2)? Actually, since the polygon is convex and vertices are given in counterclockwise order, the symmetry should preserve the order. So the symmetric vertex of vertex i should be vertex (i + N/2) % N.

For example, in a regular hexagon, vertex 0 maps to vertex 3, vertex 1 maps to vertex 4, etc.

So the algorithm is:

```python
n = int(input())
vertices = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    vertices.append((x, y))

# If N is odd, no central symmetry possible
if n % 2 == 1:
    print("NA")
else:
    half = n // 2
    midpoints = []
    valid = True
    
    for i in range(half):
        x1, y1 = vertices[i]
        x2, y2 = vertices[i + half]
        cx = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
        cy = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
        midpoints.append((cx, cy))
        
        # Check if all midpoints are the same
        if i > 0 and midpoints[0] != midpoints[i]:
            valid = False
            break
    
    if valid:
        print(f"{midpoints[0][0]:.6f} {midpoints[0][1]:.6f}")
    else:
        print("NA")
```

So the pairing (i, i+N/2) is correct.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a point P such that no matter what straight line passes through P, the areas of the two resulting convex polygons are equal. This means that every line through P divides the original polygon into two regions of equal area.

## Key Insights

1. If such a point exists, it must be the centroid of the polygon (the arithmetic mean of all vertices' coordinates).
2. For a convex polygon, if a line through a point divides it into two equal areas, that point must be the centroid.
3. However, there's a crucial insight: for a polygon that is not centrally symmetric (like a triangle), no line through a single point will always divide it into equal areas. Only in special cases (like centrally symmetric polygons) will such a point exist.

Let me think about when such a point exists:
- In a centrally symmetric polygon (like a rectangle, parallelogram, or regular polygon), the center of symmetry works.
- In an asymmetric polygon (like a triangle), there's no point where every line through it creates equal areas.

## Mathematical Analysis

For a convex polygon, the only point where every line through it divides the area equally is the centroid (center of mass). However, this is only true for centrally symmetric polygons.

Actually, let me reconsider: For a general convex polygon, is there a point such that every line through it divides the area in half?

The answer is NO for most convex polygons. The only exception is when the polygon has central symmetry (like a parallelogram, rectangle, or regular polygon with even number of sides).

For triangles (3-gon): A triangle has no central symmetry. Any line through a point will not generally divide the area equally. In fact, it's impossible for a line through any fixed point to divide a triangle into two equal areas for all possible directions.

Wait, let me be more precise: For a general polygon, there exists a point such that every line through it divides the area in half? This is actually not true. There's a well-known result in geometry that for a convex set, there exists a point that is the "centroid" and for which there exists at least one line through it that bisects the area. But we need a point where EVERY line through it bisects the area.

This is only possible if the polygon has central symmetry. In such cases, the center of symmetry works.

So the algorithm:
1. Check if the polygon has central symmetry (for every vertex, there's a corresponding vertex that is symmetric with respect to the center).
2. If it has central symmetry, the center is the answer.
3. If not, output "NA".

For a polygon with N vertices given in counterclockwise order, it has central symmetry if there exists a point C such that for each vertex V_i, the point C + (V_i - C) = V_{i+k} for some k, meaning all vertices are symmetric around C.

For a polygon to have central symmetry, N must be even, and the vertices must be paired such that each vertex i has a corresponding vertex (i + N/2) % N that is symmetric with respect to the center.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read the input
2. Check if N is even (central symmetry requires even number of vertices)
3. For even N, pair vertices i and i + N/2 (mod N) and check if they are symmetric with respect to the same center
4. Calculate the center of symmetry if it exists
5. If N is odd or the symmetry doesn't hold, output "NA"

But wait - for a triangle (N=3), no central symmetry exists, so it should output "NA" - which matches the example.

For the square example (N=4), vertices (100,100), (0,100), (0,0), (100,0) - this is a rectangle which has central symmetry at (50,50), which matches the expected output.

So the algorithm:
- If N is odd, output "NA"
- If N is even, check that opposite vertices are symmetric about their midpoint
- Find the center by averaging the coordinates of paired vertices

```python
n = int(input())
vertices = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    vertices.append((x, y))

# For odd n, no central symmetry exists
if n % 2 == 1:
    print("NA")
else:
    # For even n, check if the polygon has central symmetry
    # Each vertex i should be symmetric to vertex (i + n//2) % n
    center_x, center_y = 0.0, 0.0
    
    # Check symmetry and calculate center
    valid = True
    for i in range(n // 2):
        x1, y1 = vertices[i]
        x2, y2 = vertices[i + n // 2]
        
        # The midpoint of these two vertices should be the center
        mid_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
        mid_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
        
        # If this is not the first pair, check if all midpoints are the same
        if i == 0:
            center_x, center_y = mid_x, mid_y
        else:
            if abs(center_x - mid_x) > 1e-9 or abs(center_y - mid_y) > 1e-9:
                valid = False
                break
    
    if valid:
        print(f"{center_x:.6f} {center_y:.6f}")
    else:
        print("NA")
```

And this center must be such that the entire polygon is symmetric about it.

- For each vertex i, pair it with vertex (i + N/2) mod N (the vertex opposite to it)
- Calculate the midpoint of (vi, v(i+N/2))
- All midpoints should be the same point

   - If all midpoints are equal, output the midpoint
   - Otherwise output "NA"

```python
n = int(input())
vertices = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    vertices.append((x, y))

if n % 2 == 1:
    print("NA")
else:
    # For even n, check if centrally symmetric
    half = n // 2
    mid_x, mid_y = None, None
    
    for i in range(half):
        x1, y1 = vertices[i]
        x2, y2 = vertices[i + half]
        mid_x_i = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
        mid_y_i = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
        
        if mid_x is None:
            mid_x = mid_x_i
            mid_y = mid_y_i
        elif abs(mid_x - mid_x_i) > 1e-9 or abs(mid_y - mid_y_i) > 1e-9:
            print("NA")
            exit()
    
    print(f"{mid_x:.6f} {mid_y:.6f}")
```

A convex polygon consisting of N vertices is given. The coordinates of each vertex are represented counterclockwise by (X1, Y1), (X2, Y2), ……, (XN, YN). > N

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a point P such that any straight line passing through P divides a convex polygon into two convex polygons with equal areas.

Let me analyze the examples:

**Example 1**: A square with vertices (100,100), (0,100), (0,0), (100,0)
- The center is at (50,50)
- Any line through the center should bisect the area
- This works because of symmetry

**Example 2**: A triangle with vertices (100,100), (0,100), (0,0)
- The area is a right triangle with base 100 and height 100
- Total area = 5000
- We need a point where every line through it splits the polygon into two equal areas (2500 each)
- However, for a triangle, there's no such point - this is a known geometric fact

## Key Insights

1. **For a convex polygon**: The set of points where every line through them bisects the area is very restrictive.

2. **Centroid property**: The centroid (center of mass) of a polygon divides it into regions of equal area when connected to vertices, but not necessarily when cut by arbitrary lines.

3. **Key geometric fact**: For a convex polygon, the only point that can have every line through it bisect the area is the centroid. However, this is only true when the line can be oriented in any direction.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, there's a fundamental geometric theorem:

**Theorem**: For a convex body in 2D, there exists a line through any point that bisects the area. But the condition that *every* line through a point bisects the area is very strong.

For this to happen, the point must be such that no matter the orientation of the line through it, the areas on both sides are equal. This can only happen if the polygon has rotational symmetry or very specific properties.

In fact, for a general convex polygon, this is only possible when the polygon has a center of symmetry (like a square, rectangle, or regular polygon). 

For a triangle (which has no center of symmetry), there is no such point where every line through it bisects the area. This matches the second example where output is "NA".

## Algorithm Steps

1. Calculate the centroid (center of mass) of the polygon
2. For a general convex polygon, the centroid is the average of vertices weighted by area of triangles formed with origin
3. However, we need to verify if every line through the centroid actually bisects the area
4. For most irregular convex polygons, this won't be true
5. For symmetric shapes like squares, it will be true

Actually, let me think about this more carefully. The centroid of a polygon has the property that any line through it divides the polygon into two parts whose areas depend on the orientation of the line. There's no guarantee that all such lines produce equal areas.

But wait - there's a theorem in geometry: For any convex body, there exists at least one line through the centroid that bisects the area (this is the "Ham Sandwich Theorem" or "Pappus's centroid theorem"). However, we need a point where *every* line through it bisects the area.

After research in my knowledge base: For a convex polygon, a point has the property that every line through it bisects the area if and only if the polygon has central symmetry (i.e., it's symmetric under 180-degree rotation).

For example:
- Square: has central symmetry → such a point exists (centroid)
- Rectangle: has central symmetry → such a point exists (centroid)
- Regular polygon with even number of sides: has central symmetry → such a point exists
- Triangle: has no central symmetry → such a point does not exist

So the algorithm:

1. Check if the polygon has central symmetry
2. If it has central symmetry, return the centroid
3. If it doesn't have central symmetry, return "NA"

## How to check central symmetry?

A polygon has central symmetry if for every vertex (xi, yi), there exists another vertex (xj, yj) such that (xi + xj)/2 = cx and (yi + yj)/2 = cy, where (cx,cy) is the centroid.

More precisely: for a convex polygon with central symmetry, the centroid must be the midpoint of every pair of diametrically opposite vertices.

Steps to check central symmetry:
1. Calculate centroid (cx, cy) - the average of all vertex coordinates
2. For each vertex (xi, yi), check if there exists another vertex (xj, yj) such that (xi + xj)/2 = cx and (yi + yj)/2 = cy
3. This means xj = 2*cx - xi and yj = 2*cy - yi
4. The vertex (2*cx - xi, 2*cy - yi) must exist in the set of vertices (with proper indexing)
5. Since vertices are given counterclockwise, we can check this for each vertex

Also, we need to ensure that the mapping is consistent (each vertex maps to exactly one other vertex, and pairs are not repeated).

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    vertices = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        vertices.append((x, y))
    
    # Calculate centroid
    total_x = sum(x for x, y in vertices)
    total_y = sum(y for x, y in vertices)
    cx = total_x / n
    cy = total_y / n
    
    # Check for central symmetry
    # For each vertex (x,y), find its diametrically opposite vertex (2*cx-x, 2*cy-y)
    # This point should exist in the list of vertices
    
    vertex_set = set(vertices)
    has_central_symmetry = True
    
    for i in range(n):
        x, y = vertices[i]
        # Find the opposite point
        opposite_x = 2 * cx - x
        opposite_y = 2 * cy - y
        
        # Check if this opposite point exists in the vertex set
        opposite_point = (opposite_x, opposite_y)
        
        # Due to floating point precision, we need to be careful
        # We'll check if it's very close to any vertex
        found = False
        for j in range(n):
            x2, y2 = vertices[j]
            # Check if (x2,y2) is very close to (opposite_x, opposite_y)
            if abs(x2 - opposite_x) < 1e-5 and abs(y2 - opposite_y) < 1e-5:
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            has_central_symmetry = False
            break
    
    # Also need to ensure that we don't have self-pairing or odd number of vertices
    # For central symmetry, the number of vertices must be even (since each point pairs with another)
    if n % 2 != 0:
        has_central_symmetry = False
    
    # We also need to ensure that the pairing is consistent
    # If we have central symmetry, every vertex should pair with exactly one other
    # Let's verify that the mapping works consistently
    if has_central_symmetry:
        # For a polygon with central symmetry, the centroid is our answer
        # Format to 6 decimal places
        print(f"{cx:.6f} {cy:.6f}")
    else:
        print("NA")

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the symmetry checking. What if the polygon has central symmetry but the vertex set doesn't contain the exact opposite point due to floating point errors? And what if we have an odd number of vertices - that's impossible for central symmetry since each point must have a unique opposite.

For a central symmetry, if n is odd, it's impossible because each point must have a unique opposite point, and with n odd, we'd have one unpaired point.

So the algorithm:
1. If n is odd, return "NA"
2. Calculate centroid
3. For each vertex, find its opposite point (2*cx-x, 2*cy-y)
4. Check if this opposite point exists in the vertex set
5. If all vertices have their opposite, return centroid; else return "NA"

```python
n = int(input())
vertices = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    vertices.append((x, y))

# If n is odd, no central symmetry possible
if n % 2 != 0:
    print("NA")
else:
    # Calculate centroid
    total_x = sum(x for x, y in vertices)
    total_y = sum(y for x, y in vertices)
    cx = total_x / n
    cy = total_y / n
    
    # Create a set of vertices for O(1) lookup
    vertex_set = set(vertices)
    
    # Check central symmetry: for each vertex, its opposite should exist
    has_central_symmetry = True
    for x, y in vertices:
        opposite_x = 2 * cx - x
        opposite_y = 2 * cy - y
        
        # Check if opposite point exists in the vertex set
        opposite_point = (opposite_x, opposite_y)
        found = False
        for vx, vy in vertices:
            if abs(vx - opposite_x) < 1e-5 and abs(vy - opposite_y) < 1e-5:
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            has_central_symmetry = False
            break
    
    if has_central_symmetry:
        print(f"{cx:.6f} {cy:.6f}")
    else:
        print("NA")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find a point P such that no matter what straight line passes through P, it divides the convex polygon into two parts with equal areas.

Let me analyze what this means:

1. For any line passing through point P, the line should divide the polygon into two regions of equal area.
2. This is only possible if the point P has a very special property.

Let me think about this geometrically:
- If a line through P always divides the area in half, then P must be the centroid (center of mass) of the polygon.
- For a convex polygon, the centroid is the average of all vertices' coordinates.
- However, the centroid doesn't necessarily have the property that ANY line through it divides the area equally.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, there's a well-known geometric result: for a convex shape, there exists a line through any point that divides the area in half. But the problem asks for a point P such that EVERY line through P divides the area equally.

This is only possible if the polygon has very special symmetry. 

For example:
- In a rectangle, any line through the centroid divides it into equal areas? No, that's not true.
- Actually, a line through the centroid doesn't necessarily divide the area equally.

Let me think about this differently. For a point P to have the property that every line through it divides the polygon into two equal areas, the polygon must be centrally symmetric about P.

In a centrally symmetric polygon, for every point there's an opposite point such that the centroid is the center of symmetry. 

Looking at the examples:
- Example 1: A rectangle (100,100), (0,100), (0,0), (100,0). This is a rectangle, which is centrally symmetric about (50,50). The output is (50.00000, 50.00000).
- Example 2: A triangle (100,100), (0,100), (0,0). This is a triangle, which is not centrally symmetric. The output is "NA".

In a triangle, there's no point such that every line through it divides the area equally. This is because triangles lack central symmetry.

## Key Insight

A convex polygon has the property that every line through a point divides it into equal areas if and only if that point is the centroid AND the polygon is centrally symmetric.

But actually, I recall that for any convex body, there exists a line through the centroid that divides it into equal areas (by the ham sandwich theorem or similar), but not every line.

The condition that EVERY line through P divides the area equally is very strong. This would only happen if the polygon has central symmetry (rotational symmetry of 180 degrees).

For a centrally symmetric polygon, the centroid is the center of symmetry, and any line through the centroid will divide the polygon into two equal areas.

So the algorithm:
1. Check if the polygon is centrally symmetric
2. If it is centrally symmetric, output the centroid
3. If it isn't, output "NA"

## How to check central symmetry?

For each vertex i, there should exist a vertex j such that:
- The center of symmetry is at (cx, cy)
- (xi + xj)/2 = cx and (yi + yj)/2 = cy

So we need to find a center (cx, cy) such that for every vertex (xi, yi), there's another vertex (xj, yj) such that:
- (xi + xj)/2 = cx
- (yi + yj)/2 = cy

We can find the centroid first: cx = sum(xi)/n, cy = sum(yi)/n

Then check if for every vertex (xi, yi), there exists another vertex (xj, yj) such that:
- (xi + xj)/2 = cx → xj = 2*cx - xi
- (yi + yj)/2 = cy → yj = 2*cy - yi

And also, the pairs should be unique (each vertex should pair with exactly one other vertex).

For odd n, this is impossible because we can't pair all vertices. So only even n can have central symmetry.

Also, for each vertex, its symmetric counterpart must exist.

## Algorithm Steps:

1. Read input
2. If N is odd, output "NA" (since central symmetry requires pairing)
3. Calculate the centroid (cx, cy) = (sum(xi)/n, sum(yi)/n)
4. For each vertex (xi, yi), compute its symmetric point (2*cx - xi, 2*cy - yi)
5. Check if this symmetric point exists in the list of vertices
6. If all vertices have their symmetric counterparts (and no duplicates), output (cx, cy)
7. Otherwise, output "NA"

Wait - in the rectangle example, we have 4 vertices. Each vertex has a symmetric counterpart:
- (100,100) ↔ (0,0)
- (0,100) ↔ (100,0)
- (0,0) ↔ (100,100)
- (100,0) ↔ (0,100)

So it works.

For a triangle (3 vertices), n is odd, so it can't be centrally symmetric → "NA"

```python
n = int(input())
vertices = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    vertices.append((x, y))

# If n is odd, no central symmetry possible
if n % 2 == 1:
    print("NA")
else:
    # Calculate centroid
    total_x = sum(x for x, y in vertices)
    total_y = sum(y for x, y in vertices)
    cx = total_x / n
    cy = total_y / n
    
    # Create a set of vertices for O(1) lookup
    vertex_set = set(vertices)
    
    # Check if every vertex has its symmetric counterpart
    valid = True
    for x, y in vertices:
        # Find symmetric point
        x_sym = 2 * cx - x
        y_sym = 2 * cy - y
        sym_point = (x_sym, y_sym)
        
        # Check if symmetric point exists in the set
        if sym_point not in vertex_set:
            valid = False
            break
            
        # Also need to ensure no vertex is paired with itself
        # If (x,y) == (x_sym, y_sym), then 2*cx - x = x and 2*cy - y = y
        # This means 2*cx = 2x → cx = x, and 2*cy = 2y → cy = y
        # So the vertex (x,y) would be at the center
        # But if a vertex is at the center, its symmetric point is itself
        # This is only possible if cx = x and cy = y
        # And since vertex_set contains vertices, if (x,y) is at center, it's fine
        # We don't need to explicitly check this because the set will contain it
        # But we need to ensure each vertex is paired with a distinct other vertex
        # Actually, we're checking existence, so if a vertex pairs with itself, it's still valid
        # as long as the symmetric point exists in the set
    
    # If all vertices have symmetric counterparts, output centroid
    if valid:
        print(f"{cx:.6f} {cy:.6f}")
    else:
        print("NA")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Polycarpus has been working in the analytic department of the "F.R.A.U.D." company for as much as n days. Right now his task is to make a series of reports about the company's performance for the last n days. We know that the main information in a day report is value a_{i}, the company's profit on the i-th day. If a_{i} is negative, then the company suffered losses on the i-th day.

Polycarpus should sort the daily reports into folders. Each folder should include data on the company's performance for several consecutive days. Of course, the information on each of the n days should be exactly in one folder. Thus, Polycarpus puts information on the first few days in the first folder. The information on the several following days goes to the second folder, and so on.

It is known that the boss reads one daily report folder per day. If one folder has three or more reports for the days in which the company suffered losses (a_{i} < 0), he loses his temper and his wrath is terrible.

Therefore, Polycarpus wants to prepare the folders so that none of them contains information on three or more days with the loss, and the number of folders is minimal.

Write a program that, given sequence a_{i}, will print the minimum number of folders.


-----Input-----

The first line contains integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100), n is the number of days. The second line contains a sequence of integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (|a_{i}| ≤ 100), where a_{i} means the company profit on the i-th day. It is possible that the company has no days with the negative a_{i}.


-----Output-----

Print an integer k — the required minimum number of folders. In the second line print a sequence of integers b_1, b_2, ..., b_{k}, where b_{j} is the number of day reports in the j-th folder.

If there are multiple ways to sort the reports into k days, print any of them.


-----Examples-----
Input
11
1 2 3 -4 -5 -6 5 -5 -6 -7 6

Output
3
5 3 3 
Input
5
0 -1 100 -1 0

Output
1
5

Here goes a way to sort the reports from the first sample into three folders:  1 2 3 -4 -5 | -6 5 -5 | -6 -7 6 

In the second sample you can put all five reports in one folder.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days of company profits, where each day has a profit value `a_i`
2. We need to divide these days into consecutive folders (groups of consecutive days)
3. Each folder can contain at most 2 days with negative profits (losses)
4. We want to minimize the number of folders
5. We need to output the minimum number of folders and the sizes of each folder

## Key Insights

1. Since we want to minimize the number of folders, we should try to make each folder as large as possible
2. However, each folder can contain at most 2 negative values
3. We need to find the optimal point to break the sequence into folders
4. This is essentially a greedy problem: we try to extend each folder as long as it doesn't exceed 2 negative days

## Algorithm Design

1. Count the total number of negative days
2. If there are 0 negative days, we can put all days in one folder
3. If there are negative days, we need to distribute them across folders such that no folder has more than 2 negative days
4. The minimum number of folders needed is `ceil(total_negative_days / 2)` since each folder can hold at most 2 negative days
5. However, we also need to ensure that the consecutive constraint is satisfied (folders must be consecutive segments)

Wait, let me reconsider. The constraint is that each folder must contain consecutive days, and no folder can have 3 or more negative days.

Actually, this is a simpler greedy problem:
- We traverse the days from left to right
- We maintain a current folder that starts at some position
- We keep adding days to the current folder until it would contain 3 or more negative days
- When adding a day would make the count of negative days reach 3, we close the current folder and start a new one

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the input
2. Initialize a list to store folder sizes
3. Use a greedy approach: start from day 0, keep extending the current folder until adding the next day would make negative days ≥ 3
4. When we reach a point where adding the next day would exceed 2 negative days, close the current folder and start a new one
5. Continue until all days are processed

Let me trace through the first example:
- Days: [1, 2, 3, -4, -5, -6, 5, -5, -6, -7, 6]
- Negative days: positions 3,4,5,7,8,9 (values -4,-5,-6,-5,-6,-7)

Folder 1: start at 0
- Day 0: profit 1 (positive) → negative count = 0
- Day 1: profit 2 (positive) → negative count = 0  
- Day 2: profit 3 (positive) → negative count = 0
- Day 3: profit -4 (negative) → negative count = 1
- Day 4: profit -5 (negative) → negative count = 2
- Day 5: profit -6 (negative) → negative count = 3 → too many!

So folder 1 ends at day 4 (indices 0-4), containing days with profits [1,2,3,-4,-5]
Negative days in this folder: -4,-5 → count = 2 ✓

Folder 2: start at day 5
- Day 5: profit -6 (negative) → negative count = 1
- Day 6: profit 5 (positive) → negative count = 1
- Day 7: profit -5 (negative) → negative count = 2
- Day 8: profit -6 (negative) → negative count = 3 → too many!

So folder 2 ends at day 7 (indices 5-7), containing days with profits [-6,5,-5]
Negative days: -6,-5 → count = 2 ✓

Folder 3: start at day 8
- Day 8: profit -6 (negative) → negative count = 1
- Day 9: profit -7 (negative) → negative count = 2
- Day 10: profit 6 (positive) → negative count = 2 ✓

Folder 3 ends at day 10 (indices 8-10)

Result: folder sizes [5, 3, 3] ✓

This matches the expected output.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

folders = []
i = 0
current_negative_count = 0

while i < n:
    # Start a new folder
    start = i
    current_negative_count = 0
    
    # Extend the folder as long as we don't exceed 2 negative days
    while i < n:
        if a[i] < 0:
            current_negative_count += 1
        # If adding this day would make negative count >= 3, we need to break
        if current_negative_count >= 3:
            break
        i += 1
    
    # The folder goes from start to i-1 (inclusive)
    folder_size = i - start
    folders.append(folder_size)

print(len(folders))
print(*folders)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days of company profit data
2. We need to divide these days into consecutive folders (groups of consecutive days)
3. Each folder can contain at most 2 days with negative profit (losses)
4. We want to minimize the number of folders
5. We need to output both the minimum number of folders and the sizes of each folder

## Key Insights

1. Since we want to minimize folders, we should try to put as many days as possible in each folder
2. Each folder can have at most 2 negative values (losses)
3. We need to process days consecutively - we can't break a sequence of days
4. This is essentially a greedy problem: we go day by day and keep adding days to current folder until we reach 2 negative values, then start a new folder

## Algorithm

1. Read the input
2. Iterate through the days one by one
3. For each day, check if adding it to the current folder would make it have 3 or more negative values
4. If it would, start a new folder
5. Otherwise, add it to the current folder
6. Keep track of the folder sizes

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the first example:
- Days: [1, 2, 3, -4, -5, -6, 5, -5, -6, -7, 6]
- We need to find groups where each group has ≤ 2 negative values

Day by day:
- Day 1 (1): positive, current folder has 0 negatives → add to folder 1
- Day 2 (2): positive, current folder has 0 negatives → add to folder 1  
- Day 3 (3): positive, current folder has 0 negatives → add to folder 1
- Day 4 (-4): negative, current folder has 1 negative → add to folder 1
- Day 5 (-5): negative, current folder would have 2 negatives → add to folder 1
- Day 6 (-6): negative, current folder would have 3 negatives → start new folder 2
- Day 7 (5): positive, folder 2 has 0 negatives → add to folder 2
- Day 8 (-5): negative, folder 2 has 1 negative → add to folder 2
- Day 9 (-6): negative, folder 2 would have 2 negatives → add to folder 2
- Day 10 (-7): negative, folder 2 would have 3 negatives → start new folder 3
- Day 11 (6): positive, folder 3 has 0 negatives → add to folder 3

Result: folders of sizes [5, 3, 3] ✓

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

folders = []
current_folder_size = 0
negative_count = 0

for i in range(n):
    if a[i] < 0:
        negative_count += 1
    
    # If adding this day would make negative_count >= 3, start a new folder
    if negative_count >= 3:
        folders.append(current_folder_size)
        current_folder_size = 1
        negative_count = 1  # Reset negative count for new folder (this day is negative)
    else:
        current_folder_size += 1

# Don't forget to add the last folder
folders.append(current_folder_size)

# Output the result
print(len(folders))
print(*folders)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Polycarpus has been working in the analytic department of the "F.R.A.U.D." company for as much as n days. Right now his task is to make a series of reports about the company's performance for the last n days. We know that the main information in a day report is value a_{i}, the company's profit on the i-th day. If a_{i} is negative, then the company suffered losses on the i-th day.

Polycarpus should sort the daily reports into folders. Each folder should include data on the company's performance for several consecutive days. Of course, the information on each of the n days should be exactly in one folder. Thus, Polycarpus puts information on the first few days in the first folder. The information on the several following days goes to the second folder, and so on.

It is known that the boss reads one daily report folder per day. If one folder has three or more reports for the days in which the company suffered losses (a_{i} < 0), he loses his temper and his wrath is terrible.

Therefore, Polycarpus wants to prepare the folders so that none of them contains information on three or more days with the loss, and the number of folders is minimal.

Write a program that, given sequence a_{i}, will print the minimum number of folders.


-----Input-----

The first line contains integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100), n is the number of days. The second line contains a sequence of integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (|a_{i}| ≤ 100), where a_{i} means the company profit on the i-th day. It is possible that the company has no days with the negative a_{i}.


-----Output-----

Print an integer k — the required minimum number of folders. In the second line print a sequence of integers b_1, b_2, ..., b_{k}, where b_{j} is the number of day reports in the j-th folder.

If there are multiple ways to sort the reports into k days, print any of them.


-----Examples-----
Input
11
1 2 3 -4 -5 -6 5 -5 -6 -7 6

Output
3
5 3 3 
Input
5
0 -1 100 -1 0

Output
1
5 


-----Note-----

Here goes a way to sort the reports from the first sample into three folders:  1 2 3 -4 -5 | -6 5 -5 | -6 -7 6 

In the second sample you can put all five reports in one folder.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days of company profit data
2. Each day has a profit value `a_i` (can be negative = loss)
3. We need to divide the days into consecutive folders (groups)
4. Each folder can contain at most 2 days with negative profits (losses)
5. We want to minimize the number of folders

## Key Insights

1. Since each folder can have at most 2 days with losses, we need to group consecutive days such that no folder has 3 or more negative days.
2. We want to minimize the number of folders, so we should try to make each folder as long as possible while satisfying the constraint.
3. This is essentially a greedy problem - we can traverse the days from left to right and keep adding days to the current folder until we would exceed the limit of 2 negative days.

## Algorithm

1. Initialize folder count to 1 and current folder start index to 0
2. Traverse through each day from left to right
3. For each day, check if adding it to the current folder would create 3 or more negative days
4. If yes, start a new folder (increment folder count) and reset the current folder start
5. If no, add the day to the current folder
6. After processing all days, we have our folder sizes

## Step-by-step trace for first example:
- Days: [1, 2, 3, -4, -5, -6, 5, -5, -6, -7, 6]
- Negative days at positions: 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9 (0-indexed)
- We need to ensure no folder has 3 negative days

Let's trace:
- Start folder 1 at day 0
- Add day 0 (1): negative count = 0
- Add day 1 (2): negative count = 0  
- Add day 2 (3): negative count = 0
- Add day 3 (-4): negative count = 1
- Add day 4 (-5): negative count = 2
- Add day 5 (-6): negative count = 3 → too many! Start new folder

So folder 1: days 0-4 (5 days), folder 2: starts at day 5
- Folder 2: day 5 (-6): negative count = 1
- Add day 6 (5): negative count = 1
- Add day 7 (-5): negative count = 2
- Add day 8 (-6): negative count = 3 → too many! Start new folder

So folder 2: days 5-7 (3 days), folder 3: starts at day 8
- Folder 3: day 8 (-6): negative count = 1
- Add day 9 (-7): negative count = 2
- Add day 10 (6): negative count = 2 → valid

Final: folders of size 5, 3, 3 → matches expected output!

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

folders = []
current_folder_start = 0
negative_count = 0

for i in range(n):
    # Check if adding current day would exceed 2 negative days
    if a[i] < 0:
        negative_count += 1
    
    # If we would have 3 or more negative days in current folder, start new folder
    if negative_count >= 3:
        folders.append(i - current_folder_start + 1)  # Add size of current folder
        current_folder_start = i
        negative_count = 1  # Reset negative count for new folder (current day is negative)
    # If we're at the end, add the last folder
    # We'll handle this at the end

# Don't forget the last folder
if current_folder_start < n:
    folders.append(n - current_folder_start)

print(len(folders))
print(' '.join(map(str, folders)))
```

Wait, I need to fix the logic when we start a new folder. When we start a new folder, we should only count the current negative day and not reset to 0.

Let me correct:

```python
        folders.append(i - current_folder_start + 1)
        # Start new folder
        current_folder_start = i
        # Reset negative count for new folder (only counts current negative day)
        negative_count = 1
    
# Add the last folder

    folders.append(n - current_folder_start)

print(len(folders))
print(' '.join(map(str, folders)))
```

But actually, when we start a new folder at position i, the current day a[i] is negative, so negative_count should be 1.

- At i=1: negative_count=1 → continue

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days of company performance data with profits/losses represented by integers
2. We need to split these days into consecutive folders (contiguous subarrays)
3. Each folder can contain at most 2 days with negative values (losses)
4. We want to minimize the number of folders
5. We need to output the minimum number of folders and the size of each folder

## Key Insights

1. Since each folder can have at most 2 negative values, we need to group consecutive days such that no group has 3 or more negative days
2. To minimize folders, we should maximize the size of each folder while respecting the constraint
3. This is essentially a greedy problem: we traverse the days and keep adding days to the current folder until we would exceed 2 negative values
4. When we hit 3 negative values, we must start a new folder

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read the input: `n` and the array of profits
2. Initialize variables:
   - `folders = []` to store the size of each folder
   - `current_folder_size = 0` to track current folder size
   - `negative_count = 0` to track negative values in current folder
3. Iterate through each day:
   - If the current day has negative profit, increment `negative_count`
   - If `negative_count` reaches 3, we need to start a new folder
   - Add the current day to current folder
4. When we need to start a new folder, append the current folder size to result and reset current folder size and negative count
5. After the loop, append the remaining days in the last folder
6. Return the number of folders and their sizes

Wait, let me reconsider this. The constraint is that no folder should have 3 or more days with negative values. So we should stop adding days when adding the next day would make the negative count reach 3.

Actually, the greedy approach is: keep adding days to the current folder until we would exceed 2 negative values if we added the next day. But since we're processing sequentially, we should stop when the current accumulated negative count + next day's negative count would be >= 3.

Let me trace through the first example:
- Days: [1, 2, 3, -4, -5, -6, 5, -5, -6, -7, 6]
- Negative days at positions: 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9 (0-indexed)

We need to group so no group has 3 negative values.

Greedy approach:
- Start with first folder: add days until we have 2 negative values or can't add more without exceeding 2 negatives
- Day 0: 1 (positive) → 0 negatives
- Day 1: 2 (positive) → 0 negatives  
- Day 2: 3 (positive) → 0 negatives
- Day 3: -4 (negative) → 1 negative
- Day 4: -5 (negative) → 2 negatives
- Day 5: -6 (negative) → would make 3 negatives, so stop here
- First folder: days 0-4 → [1,2,3,-4,-5] → 2 negatives ✓

Second folder starts at day 5:
- Day 5: -6 (negative) → 1 negative
- Day 6: 5 (positive) → 1 negative
- Day 7: -5 (negative) → 2 negatives
- Day 8: -6 (negative) → would make 3 negatives, so stop
- Second folder: days 5-7 → [-6,5,-5] → 2 negatives ✓

Wait, this gives us 3 folders: [0-4], [5-7], and then remaining days 8-10: [-6,-7,6] → 2 negatives ✓

Actually, the example shows 5,3,3 which means:
- Folder 1: 5 days (days 0-4): 1,2,3,-4,-5 → 2 negatives
- Folder 2: 3 days (days 5-7): -6,5,-5 → 2 negatives  
- Folder 3: 3 days (days 8-10): -6,-7,6 → 2 negatives

This works.

My algorithm:
1. Initialize folder sizes list
2. Start with current folder
3. For each day from 0 to n-1:
   - Add day to current folder
   - Count negative values in current folder
   - If negative_count >= 3, we need to start a new folder
   - So when we encounter a day that would make negative_count >= 3, we finish current folder and start a new one
4. After processing all days, add the last folder

Actually, we should check: when adding the current day, if negative_count would become >= 3, then we cannot add this day to current folder.

So the algorithm:
- current_folder_start = 0
- folder_sizes = []
- negative_count = 0

For each day i from 0 to n-1:
- If a[i] < 0: negative_count += 1
- If negative_count >= 3: 
  - Finish current folder from current_folder_start to i-1
  - Add (i - current_folder_start) to folder_sizes
  - Reset current_folder_start = i
  - Reset negative_count = 1 if a[i] < 0 else 0
- Otherwise: continue adding to current folder

Wait, when negative_count >= 3, it means adding day i would make it >= 3, so we need to stop before adding day i.

Actually, we need to check if adding day i would make negative_count >= 3.

So:
- For day i, if current negative_count + (1 if a[i] < 0 else 0) >= 3, then we must end the current folder before day i
- So we add the current folder (from folder_start to i-1) and start a new folder at i

Let me implement this:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

folder_sizes = []
current_start = 0
negative_count = 0

for i in range(n):
    if a[i] < 0:
        negative_count += 1
    
    # Check if adding this day would make negative_count >= 3
    if negative_count >= 3:
        # Need to end the current folder at position i-1
        folder_sizes.append(i - current_start)
        current_start = i
        negative_count = 1 if a[i] < 0 else 0
    # If negative_count < 3, we continue to next day

# Don't forget the last folder
folder_sizes.append(n - current_start)

print(len(folder_sizes))
print(*folder_sizes)
```

Wait, this has a bug. When we add a day, we first increment negative_count, then check if it's >= 3. But if a[i] is negative, we increment first, then check.

For example, if current negative_count is 2, and a[i] is negative, then after increment it becomes 3, which triggers the condition. This means we add a folder from current_start to i-1, then start new folder at i with negative_count = 1.

But what if current negative_count is already 2, and a[i] is positive? Then we don't increment, negative_count remains 2, and we continue.

Actually, the logic seems correct. Let me trace through the first example:
- a = [1,2,3,-4,-5,-6,5,-5,-6,-7,6]

i=0: a[0]=1 (positive), negative_count=0 < 3 → continue
i=1: a[1]=2 (positive), negative_count=0 < 3 → continue  
i=2: a[2]=3 (positive), negative_count=0 < 3 → continue
i=3: a[3]=-4 (negative), negative_count=1 < 3 → continue
i=4: a[4]=-5 (negative), negative_count=2 < 3 → continue
i=5: a[5]=-6 (negative), negative_count=3 >= 3 → finish current folder from 0 to 4, size = 5, start new folder at 5, negative_count = 1
i=6: a[6]=5 (positive), negative_count=1 < 3 → continue
i=7: a[7]=-5 (negative), negative_count=2 < 3 → continue
i=8: a[8]=-6 (negative), negative_count=3 >= 3 → finish current folder from 5 to 7, size = 3, start new folder at 8, negative_count = 1
i=9: a[9]=-7 (negative), negative_count=2 < 3 → continue
i=10: a[10]=6 (positive), negative_count=2 < 3 → continue

After loop: add last folder from 8 to 10, size = 3

So folder_sizes = [5, 3, 3] → correct!

Let me check the second example:
- a = [0,-1,100,-1,0]

i=0: a[0]=0, negative_count=0 → continue
i=1: a[1]=-1, negative_count=1 → continue
i=2: a[2]=100, negative_count=1 → continue
i=3: a[3]=-1, negative_count=2 → continue  
i=4: a[4]=0, negative_count=2 → continue

No trigger, so folder_sizes = [5], then add last folder of size 5.

Output: 1 folder of size 5 → correct!

The algorithm works!

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

folder_sizes = []
current_start = 0
negative_count = 0

for i in range(n):
    if a[i] < 0:
        negative_count += 1
    
    if negative_count >= 3:
        folder_sizes.append(i - current_start)
        current_start = i
        negative_count = 1 if a[i] < 0 else 0

folder_sizes.append(n - current_start)

print(len(folder_sizes))
print(*folder_sizes)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` days of company profit data
2. We need to divide these days into consecutive folders (groups)
3. Each folder can contain at most 2 days with negative profits (losses)
4. We want to minimize the number of folders
5. We need to output the minimum number of folders and the size of each folder

## Key Insights

1. A folder can have at most 2 negative values (losses)
2. We want to minimize the number of folders, so we should try to maximize the number of days in each folder
3. We can use a greedy approach: scan through the days and keep adding days to the current folder until we would exceed 2 negative values
4. When adding a day would make the negative count in a folder exceed 2, we start a new folder

## Algorithm

1. Read the input
2. Use a greedy approach to form folders:
   - Start with the first folder
   - Keep adding days to the current folder as long as the number of negative values in that folder is less than 3
   - When adding the next day would make negative count ≥ 3, start a new folder
3. Record the sizes of each folder
4. Output the number of folders and the sizes

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the first example:
- Days: [1, 2, 3, -4, -5, -6, 5, -5, -6, -7, 6]
- Day 1: 1 (positive) - current negative count = 0
- Day 2: 2 (positive) - current negative count = 0  
- Day 3: 3 (positive) - current negative count = 0
- Day 4: -4 (negative) - current negative count = 1
- Day 5: -5 (negative) - current negative count = 2
- Day 6: -6 (negative) - would make count = 3 (not allowed), so start new folder
- Folder 1: days 1-5 (values: 1,2,3,-4,-5) with 2 negatives
- Folder 2: day 6 (value: -6) with 1 negative
- Day 7: 5 (positive) - current negative = 0
- Day 8: -5 (negative) - current negative = 1
- Day 9: -6 (negative) - would make count = 2 (allowed)
- Day 10: -7 (negative) - would make count = 3 (not allowed), so start new folder
- Folder 3: days 7-9 (values: 5,-5,-6) with 2 negatives
- Folder 4: day 10 (value: -7) with 1 negative
- Day 11: 6 (positive) - current negative = 0
- This would make 4 folders, but the expected is 3.

Wait, let me recheck the expected output: "5 3 3" meaning 3 folders of sizes 5, 3, 3.

Let me verify:
- Folder 1: days 1-5: [1,2,3,-4,-5] → negatives: -4,-5 → count = 2 ✓
- Folder 2: days 6-8: [-6,5,-5] → negatives: -6,-5 → count = 2 ✓  
- Folder 3: days 9-11: [-6,-7,6] → negatives: -6,-7 → count = 2 ✓

So the greedy approach needs to be more careful. The key insight is that we need to avoid creating folders with 3 or more negatives, and we want to minimize the total number of folders.

Actually, the greedy approach should work: when we encounter a negative value that would make the current folder have 3 negatives, we must start a new folder.

Let me implement the greedy algorithm properly:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

folders = []
current_folder = []
negative_count = 0

for day in range(n):
    if a[day] < 0:
        negative_count += 1
    
    # If adding this day would make negative_count >= 3, start a new folder
    if negative_count >= 3:
        folders.append(len(current_folder))
        current_folder = []
        negative_count = 1 if a[day] < 0 else 0
    else:
        current_folder.append(a[day])

# Don't forget the last folder
folders.append(len(current_folder))

# But wait, this doesn't work perfectly because we're counting negative_count incorrectly
```

The issue is that when we start a new folder, we should reset the negative count, not just the accumulation.

Let me correct the algorithm:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

folders = []
current_folder = []
negative_count = 0

for i in range(n):
    if a[i] < 0:
        negative_count += 1
    
    # If the current folder would have 3 or more negatives when we add this day
    if negative_count >= 3:
        # Finish the current folder and start a new one
        folders.append(len(current_folder))
        current_folder = []
        negative_count = 1 if a[i] < 0 else 0
    else:
        current_folder.append(a[i])

# Add the last folder
folders.append(len(current_folder))

# But this might result in an incorrect count because negative_count is not properly reset
# Actually, when we reset negative_count, we only count the current negative value
```

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me think simpler:

The condition is: we can't have 3 or more negative values in any folder. So when adding a negative day, if it would make the number of negative days in the current folder reach 3, we must split.

In the loop:
- We add the current day to current_folder
- We increment negative_count if a[i] < 0
- If negative_count >= 3, we finish the current folder and start a new one (resetting negative_count to 0, then increment by 1 if current day is negative)

But actually, when we start a new folder, we should just have the current day in it, so negative_count should be just 1 if a[i] < 0.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Polycarpus has been working in the analytic department of the "F.R.A.U.D." company for as much as n days. Right now his task is to make a series of reports about the company's performance for the last n days. We know that the main information in a day report is value a_{i}, the company's profit on the i-th day. If a_{i} is negative, then the company suffered losses on the i-th day.

Polycarpus should sort the daily reports into folders. Each folder should include data on the company's performance for several consecutive days. Of course, the information on each of the n days should be exactly in one folder. Thus, Polycarpus puts information on the first few days in the first folder. The information on the several following days goes to the second folder, and so on.

It is known that the boss reads one daily report folder per day. If one folder has three or more reports for the days in which the company suffered losses (a_{i} < 0), he loses his temper and his wrath is terrible.

Therefore, Polycarpus wants to prepare the folders so that none of them contains information on three or more days with the loss, and the number of folders is minimal.

Write a program that, given sequence a_{i}, will print the minimum number of folders.


-----Input-----

The first line contains integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100), n is the number of days. The second line contains a sequence of integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (|a_{i}| ≤ 100), where a_{i} means the company profit on the i-th day. It is possible that the company has no days with the negative a_{i}.


-----Output-----

Print an integer k — the required minimum number of folders. In the second line print a sequence of integers b_1, b_2, ..., b_{k}, where b_{j} is the number of day reports in the j-th folder.

If there are multiple ways to sort the reports into k days, print any of them.


-----Examples-----
Input
11
1 2 3 -4 -5 -6 5 -5 -6 -7 6

Output
3
5 3 3 
Input
5
0 -1 100 -1 0

Output
1
5 


-----Note-----

In the second sample you can put all five reports in one folder.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout
